After a five-year probe, Rundle concluded there wasn’t enough evidence, even though witnesses heard Rainey screaming in agony; this decision provoked public outrage, with Miami-Dade Democrats urging her resignation for “refus[ing] to prosecute Darren Rainey’s killers”【15†L255-L264】【18†L126-L134】.

In fact, as of 2017, **she had never charged a police officer for an on-duty killing in 24 years as state attorney**, a record the Miami New Times labeled a “disgrace”【11†L146-L154】. - **Prosecutorial Misconduct in Her Office:** Recent investigations have exposed **ethical lapses by prosecutors under Rundle’s administration**.

In 2023, a veteran homicide prosecutor, **Michael Von Zamft**, resigned after a judge found he and another prosecutor **manipulated witness testimony** to sway a death penalty case【9†L373-L381】.

It was later revealed the state attorney’s office had for years been using a **convicted murderer as a jailhouse informant** – arranging “manufactured” encounters where he elicited confessions from other inmates – in exchange for a lenient deal on his own murders【9†L379-L388】【9†L398-L406】.

Legal experts called this **improper and likely unconstitutional**, prompting the Florida Association of Criminal Defense Lawyers to demand an independent integrity unit to oversee Rundle’s office【9†L407-L415】.

Rundle conceded to consider the proposal amid mounting criticism of **“rogue” tactics by her prosecutors**【7†L339-L347】【9†L411-L419】. - **Threats Against a Police Critic:** In 2014, Rundle’s office drew fire for **threatening a citizen with prosecution with no legal basis**.

After police accountability activist James Eric McDonough recorded a meeting with a police chief (in a public setting) and posted it online, **Rundle sent him a cease-and-desist letter claiming his recording was a felony** and warning she could prosecute if he did it again【18†L84-L92】.

McDonough sued, and a federal appeals court in 2017 ruled Rundle **“had no basis in the law”** for her threat – the meeting wasn’t private, so no law was broken【16†L33-L41】【18†L103-L112】.

The court allowed McDonough’s civil rights lawsuit to proceed, calling Rundle’s actions an overreach that **violated First Amendment protections**【18†L113-L118】. - **Other Ethics and Legal Complaints:** Fernandez Rundle’s hardline tactics have spurred broader calls for accountability.

In 2017, after community backlash over the Rainey case, she faced a **non-binding resolution by the Miami-Dade Democratic Executive Committee urging her to resign** for failing to pursue justice in cases of official misconduct【18†L126-L134】.

However, formal ethics charges against Rundle herself are scarce.

One **ethics complaint (2019)** accused her of using her position to harass a private citizen (who was in a lawsuit against Rundle’s family), but **Miami-Dade’s Ethics Commission dismissed it as legally insufficient** – the complainant provided no evidence that Rundle misused her office【26†L7-L15】【26†L23-L31】.

Ultimately, while Rundle has never been criminally charged, these **controversies highlight patterns of perceived leniency toward officials’ misdeeds and aggressive tactics against critics**, raising persistent questions about her office’s integrity【11†L146-L154】【9†L407-L415】. ## Daniella Levine Cava (Miami-Dade Mayor) - **Dismissed Ethics Complaint (2017):** Before becoming Mayor, Levine Cava served on the County Commission, where she faced at least one ethics inquiry.

In 2017 a private citizen filed a complaint accusing her of **“harassing” or intimidating him at a public event**, allegedly leveraging her position【26†L7-L15】.

The Miami-Dade Ethics Commission found **no factual basis** for misuse of office and **dismissed the complaint as not legally sufficient** on May 10, 2017【26†L17-L25】【26†L27-L33】.

In short, the allegation was deemed unfounded. - **Procurement Controversy and Lobbying Scandal (2021):** Early in her mayoral term, Levine Cava came under scrutiny for a **major county contract decision** at Miami International Airport.

This unusual move raised eyebrows, and a local watchdog blog exposed that it followed intense lobbying by former county officials and well-connected lobbyists.

After reports that Bravo’s behind-the-scenes involvement **may have violated ethics laws**, and that lobbyists (including a prominent fundraiser close to the Mayor) were pressuring for a do-over, Levine Cava **abruptly reversed course**【33†L93-L100】【33†L125-L133】.

Levine Cava’s backtrack effectively ended the immediate controversy, but the episode highlighted **concerns about insider influence and transparency** in her administration’s dealings. - **Calusa Housing Project Rezoning Dispute (2021):** Levine Cava also became embroiled in a heated zoning controversy over the **“Save Calusa” development fight**.

In late 2021, Miami-Dade County (led by Levine Cava and the Commission) approved a rezoning to allow 550 homes on the former Calusa golf course – land that had been intended as green space【34†L149-L158】.

Local residents opposed to the development filed an **ethics complaint** claiming the decisive zoning hearing on Nov. 17, 2021 was **improperly noticed in violation of county rules**【34†L157-L165】.

County code requires newspaper notice 14 days in advance; opponents allege that when the hearing date changed, the county failed to publish a new notice in time【34†L159-L168】.

The County insists it **followed all notice requirements**, providing courtesy emails and mailers, though the e-mail went out less than 14 days prior【34†L169-L177】.

While this is a procedural dispute (no personal wrongdoing by the Mayor was alleged), it has fueled a **pending legal challenge** and criticism that the county rushed through a favor to the developer at the expense of public input【34†L153-L161】【34†L167-L175】.

Her challenger, Shlomo Danzinger (a former Surfside mayor), sued Miami-Dade County claiming **Levine Cava manipulated how her name appeared on the ballot** – effectively **committing election fraud to secure the top placement**【27†L75-L83】.

According to the complaint, her team submitted two different oaths of candidacy on the same day (May 23, 2024): one in the morning listing her surname as “Levine Cava” (which, alphabetically, would have placed her 4th among seven mayoral candidates), and then a second oath later that day listing her last name as just “Cava,” ensuring she would appear **first on the ballot**【27†L85-L94】.

Danzinger argues this double-filing was illegal and seeks to invalidate her candidacy【27†L79-L87】【27†L93-L100】.

Notably, Levine Cava herself is **not named as a defendant** in the suit (it targets the county and election officials), and her campaign dismissed the move as a baseless “stunt” on the eve of the primary【27†L73-L81】.

Thus far, no court has validated those fraud allegations. - **Financial and Ethical Conduct:** Overall, **no criminal charges or major personal scandals** have surfaced against Levine Cava in her career.

Her decades of nonprofit work and public service have been free of known financial misconduct.

However, the incidents above have subjected her to **ethics probes and public criticism**.

Levine Cava has generally responded by cooperating with investigators or reversing disputed decisions, and the county’s Ethics Commission **cleared her of any intentional wrongdoing in the complaints it reviewed**【26†L27-L33】.

Still, these controversies are frequently cited by opponents to question her judgment and commitment to ethical governance. ## Ron DeSantis (Florida Governor) - **Allegations from Military Service (Guantánamo Bay):** Even before entering politics, DeSantis’s conduct came under fire from a human rights perspective.

These claims surfaced via an unaired VICE documentary transcript and were reported in the press, though DeSantis **has not faced any formal investigation or charges** over his Gitmo tenure.

In 2022 he made national headlines by suspending **Hillsborough County State Attorney Andrew Warren** – an elected prosecutor – after Warren publicly said he wouldn’t enforce laws criminalizing abortion or gender-affirming care【48†L169-L177】【48†L185-L193】.

Warren sued, arguing this was retaliation for protected speech.

A federal judge found that **some of DeSantis’s reasons for removal did violate Warren’s First Amendment rights**, essentially **confirming the suspension was partly political**【48†L189-L197】.

DeSantis doubled down in 2023 by ousting another locally elected prosecutor, **Orlando-area State Attorney Monique Worrell**, accusing her of “leniency” in prosecutions.

Civil rights groups noted the grounds were **“unsubstantiated”** and decried the move as **overturning voters’ will for political gain**【56†L79-L88】【56†L91-L99】. (Worrell ran for her office again and was re-elected by voters in 2024 despite the Governor’s actions.) DeSantis’s willingness to remove or override other officials has prompted widespread debate and litigation, with critics calling it **an abuse of power** and his administration insisting it’s upholding law and order.

Similarly, DeSantis became embroiled in a feud with **The Walt Disney Company** after it opposed his 2022 “Parental Rights in Education” law.

Disney responded with a high-profile lawsuit accusing DeSantis of **political retaliation that violated the First Amendment**, saying he weaponized state power to punish Disney’s speech【54†L11-L19】. (A federal judge later dismissed Disney’s case on jurisdictional grounds, but the clash remains a potent example of DeSantis’s confrontational style.) These incidents collectively have raised red flags for watchdogs, who argue DeSantis **blurs the line between governing and retribution**, potentially setting dangerous precedents. - **Migrant Relocation Program – Legal Challenges:** One of the most controversial projects of DeSantis’s tenure is his **migrant relocation program**, in which Florida officials – using state funds – transported Latin American asylum-seekers from Texas to northern states without clear notice.

In Texas, **Bexar County Sheriff Javier Salazar completed a criminal probe** into the incident and in 2023 formally **recommended that the local district attorney pursue charges** for unlawful restraint (kidnapping) related to how migrants were lured onto the flights【51†L128-L136】【51†L139-L147】.

According to the sheriff’s report, operatives allegedly misled the migrants with **“false promises” of work and assistance** if they boarded the plane【51†L133-L141】.

The case has been referred to Bexar County prosecutors and includes both misdemeanor and felony counts of Unlawful Restraint【51†L139-L147】.

While **no charges have been filed yet against specific individuals**, the investigation suggested potential criminal implications for those running Florida’s program.

Additionally, civil rights groups have sued Florida in federal court, calling the relocations an **unconstitutional deprivation of migrants’ rights** (those cases are ongoing).

DeSantis staunchly defends the relocations as voluntary and lawful, but the **unorthodox use of state funds (some of which were COVID-relief dollars) and possible fraud in execution** have drawn federal scrutiny.

Treasury’s Inspector General is auditing whether Florida misused pandemic funds for this program, and Democratic officials in Washington have urged a DOJ investigation.

In sum, the migrant flights scheme has become a legal minefield – **an active Texas criminal case and multiple probes** could yet entangle members of DeSantis’s team, though DeSantis himself maintains he broke no laws【51†L133-L141】【51†L149-L156】. - **Ethics, Transparency, and Financial Conduct:** Governor DeSantis’s meteoric rise has been accompanied by questions about **ethics and financial disclosures**.

In 2023, a nonpartisan watchdog group filed a detailed ethics **complaint accusing DeSantis of failing to report expensive gifts** related to his political travel.

The Campaign Legal Center alleges that a conservative nonprofit organization paid for DeSantis to fly on private jets to at least eight states for speeches and book tour events, yet **he did not disclose these charter flights as gifts** on his required financial reports【53†L49-L57】【53†L63-L72】.

Florida law treats third-party paid travel above $100 as a reportable gift, and the private jet trips would easily qualify, meaning the omission **“appears to be a clear violation of state laws”** according to the complaint【53†L63-L71】.

DeSantis’s team contends these were legitimate business or campaign-related trips, not gifts, and the Florida Ethics Commission (whose members are appointed by the governor and legislature) has so far **not taken action**.

In fact, an earlier ethics complaint filed by a Trump-aligned PAC – which accused DeSantis of running a “shadow presidential campaign” using his state office for leverage – was **dismissed by the Ethics Commission in May 2023** for “no legal basis”【41†L128-L136】【41†L138-L145】. (Notably, that commission is largely populated by DeSantis’s allies【41†L138-L142】, raising concerns about impartiality.) Separately, DeSantis faced criticism for **using state resources for personal political objectives**, such as the Florida Department of Law Enforcement ferrying him to campaign events, and a dubious $1.25 million state contract with a jet company that coincided with his out-of-state tour (reported by Florida media).

In response to these scrutiny attempts, DeSantis quietly **signed a new law in 2023 curbing the powers of state ethics boards** – imposing shorter investigation time limits and stricter complaint requirements, which watchdogs blasted as **“undermining…50 years of ethics oversight”** in Florida【43†L1-L9】【43†L21-L25】.

While DeSantis has not been found guilty of any wrongdoing and has avoided any personal criminal or civil penalties, the **numerous ethics complaints and rule changes** indicate an environment where **transparency is often lacking and accountability mechanisms are being tested**. - **Associates and Administration Ties:** Several **officials and allies linked to DeSantis have had legal troubles**, reflecting on his administration’s circle.

In December 2022, Stermon was found dead by suicide in his car – and authorities revealed he had been the target of an **“active investigation” by Jacksonville police into alleged sexual misconduct** at the time of his death【45†L133-L142】【45†L143-L152】.

According to law enforcement leaks, Stermon was accused of coercing young women (including an underaged teen) in exchange for favors, a serious scandal that never reached prosecution due to his death【44†L33-L41】.

DeSantis’s office stated they had “no knowledge” of the investigation and were shocked by Stermon’s death【45†L153-L160】.

Additionally, venues and artists negotiate merchandise rights (venues often take a cut of t-shirt and album sales at the show).

Police were often overwhelmed trying to control surges of teenage fans, and concerts sometimes saw fainting, trampling, or riots.

There’s the thrill of hearing a playback of a take and realizing *“yes, that’s the one!”* It’s common for musicians to talk about getting “goosebumps” in the control room when a perfect vocal or guitar solo is captured. **Producers and Engineers** will sometimes employ tricks to get the best out of artists.

In exchange, the artist typically signs away a portion of their earnings and some rights.

A real-world illustration: the R&B group TLC sold multi-millions of records in the ’90s but infamously declared bankruptcy due to a contract that left them with very little per album sold after recoupment.

In 1976 a court ruled their manager, Don Arden, owed them £12,000 in unpaid royalties【22†L193-L200】 – not a small sum for that time.

Rock history also has figures like Allen Klein (who managed The Beatles and The Rolling Stones at different points) – Klein did secure higher royalties for his clients but also ended up owning rights to many of their songs and was later accused of pocketing more than his fair share. **Major Industry Scams:** Sometimes the exploitation is outright criminal.

He amassed over **$300 million through fraud**, and his artists found themselves hugely popular but underpaid【24†L134-L142】【24†L138-L140】.

In 2006, Pearlman was finally sued and exposed for misrepresentation and fraud【24†L134-L142】.

The **1970s payola scandals** (record labels bribing radio DJs to play songs) were a form of industry corruption – not directly hurting musicians financially, but affecting which songs became hits through illicit means.

These struggles underscore a key point: many record deals historically were very one-sided in favor of the label, and only the most successful artists had the clout to renegotiate or fight back. **How Bands Get Exploited:** Besides contracts, rock bands can be exploited through *publishing* (songwriting rights) and *merchandise*.

A dramatic example: Creedence Clearwater Revival’s John Fogerty wrote all their hits, but in a twisted contract situation, he eventually lost the rights to his own songs to his publisher (their label boss Saul Zaentz).

The Rolling Stones formed **Rolling Stones Records** in 1970 after their contract with Decca ended, to have more control (though they still partnered with major labels for distribution).

The *impostor syndrome* (feeling like a fraud despite success) has been reported by various famous artists.

Many classic albums are in part a documentation of an artist’s state of mind, whether drug-fueled or depressed.

In rock, sometimes scandal boosts the mythology. - **Altamont (again):** The death at Altamont in ’69 we discussed was exacerbated by drugs – the Hells Angels were reportedly drinking and allegedly on drugs, the crowd too, creating a volatile atmosphere where judgment was impaired and violence erupted. - **Arrests and legal troubles:** Many rock stars have been arrested for drug possession (Rolling Stones members in 1967, Led Zeppelin members had various run-ins, etc.), which often became high-profile news.

So, a mid-level rock band might see only a few hundred dollars from a song that got 1 million streams if they don’t own all the rights.

If their next records flop, they could be in trouble financially but still have the lifestyle inertia of big spenders.

Billy Joel (more soft rock) discovered in 1989 that his manager (and ex brother-in-law) had misappropriated tens of millions of dollars; he sued and won some damages, but it was a lesson that even superstars need to watch their books. - **Legal disputes**: Suing or being sued can drain finances.

Black Sabbath’s Tony Iommi went through lawsuits over the band name and rights.

Legal fees mount up, and royalties can be frozen during disputes.

Debt restructurings in court (Chapter 11) have happened – e.g., Meat Loaf filed for bankruptcy in 1983 after a songwriter sued him for unpaid royalties; he later paid off debts after his comeback with *Bat Out of Hell II* in 1993.

Also, sometimes the violence comes from the musician: e.g., **Sid Vicious of the Sex Pistols** infamously hit an audience member with his bass guitar; or **Jim Morrison** allegedly inciting a “riot” with his wild behavior (leading to the Miami charges).

In more modern times, these behaviors are re-evaluated; some rock stars have faced the #MeToo spotlight for historical misconduct.

A famous event: **Jim Morrison’s alleged indecent exposure (1969 Miami)** as mentioned, led to a trial and The Doors being blacklisted from some radio and concerts for a while【82†L5-L12】.

Bands like Judas Priest and Ozzy Osbourne were even taken to court over suicides where their music was blamed (e.g., the 1990 Judas Priest trial alleging hidden “do it” messages in a song – they were cleared).

Others, like Marilyn Manson in the late 90s, *embraced* being the villain in the media, which ironically increased his allure to fans but made him a scapegoat (notably, he was blamed by some for the Columbine school shooting in 1999 with zero evidence, leading to canceled shows). - **Crime and Legal Trouble:** When a rock star gets arrested or convicted, it’s big news. *Examples:* The Rolling Stones’ Keith Richards was arrested in 1977 in Canada for heroin – he faced possible serious prison, which would end the Stones; he got off with a rehab and a benefit concert deal.

Similarly, punk rocker Sid Vicious was charged with murdering his girlfriend Nancy Spungen in 1978 – he died of overdose awaiting trial; the lurid tale became part of the punk rock lore and spurred films. - **Inter-band Drama in Public:** Sometimes the scandal is internal disputes made public.

Career Killer**: Some controversies undoubtedly helped sell records.

That rebellious aura was central to their appeal.

The term *Beatlemania* was coined because fans (mostly young women) would literally mob The Beatles – at airports, outside hotels, at concerts (leading to scenes like police with water hoses controlling crowds【81†L442-L450】【81†L450-L458】).

The Beatles’ fan club even got special Christmas records mailed to them【81†L474-L482】. - Autograph signing was big.

The ’70s were relatively flush economically for the industry (tons of records sold on vinyl, high concert ticket demand).

Conversely, fans also appreciate when rock stars use their platform positively (like speaking for social justice, which many do). - **Changing Notion of ‘Rock Star’:** The term “rock star” has even broadened to mean anyone at the top of their field (like referring to a “rock star programmer”).

It is a call for immediate investigation, intervention, and accountability. --- ### **Background on New Hope C.O.R.P.S.** New Hope C.O.R.P.S., established in 1993, is a CARF-accredited nonprofit providing substance abuse treatment, behavioral health services, and case management.

Sponsored by Thriving Mind South Florida and the Florida Department of Children and Families, New Hope has contracted with the Miami-Dade County Homeless Trust since 1999, aiming to serve approximately 120 homeless individuals annually.

Despite its stated track record, our experience reveals a starkly different reality. --- ### **Instances of False Hope and Misconduct by New Hope C.O.R.P.S.** Our interactions with New Hope were marked by a series of unfulfilled promises, negligence, and deliberate obfuscation: 1. **Dee-Dee’s Initial Engagement and Abandonment** Dee-Dee approached us in late 2021 with promises of expedited housing and enrollment in a Suboxone program.

She also provided conflicting information about housing funds, initially stating they would be available in May 2022, then changing her story to September 2022. 2. **Adam’s Empty Promises** After Dee-Dee vanished, Adam stepped in, promising to rectify past failures.

He assured us that housing vouchers were imminent, but upon contacting Taylor, who was supposedly handling the vouchers, we were met with confusion and denial.

Taylor claimed no knowledge of Stephen or the Rapid Re-Housing program.

She not only denied involvement with Stephen but feigned ignorance of the Rapid Re-Housing program altogether.

When confronted publicly, she removed our social media posts, effectively silencing our attempts to seek accountability. 5. **The Illegal Eviction in January 2025** A Miami-Dade Police Department sergeant visited our encampment, claiming to assist with housing through New Hope.

Days later, Stephen Heath posted a five-day eviction notice on our shelter.

This action violated Florida Statute §48.021, which restricts the service of legal notices to sheriffs or certified process servers—neither of which Stephen is.

Furthermore, Florida law mandates court involvement for eviction proceedings, which was entirely bypassed in our case.

Our belongings were discarded by workers after Officer Cheney denied us access when we took a short break, causing us to lose essential items and suffer profound emotional distress. --- ### **Legal Violations and Misconduct** The actions taken against us constitute clear violations of Florida law: - **Unlawful Eviction:** The eviction lacked proper notice, court authorization, and involvement of certified process servers as required by Florida law. - **Unauthorized Practice:** Stephen Heath’s actions in posting the eviction notice exceeded his legal authority. - **Collusion and Misrepresentation:** New Hope’s collaboration with MDPD, under the guise of assistance, aimed to displace us without due process. --- ### **Corroborating Evidence from Formal Complaints** The following complaints corroborate our experiences and highlight systemic issues: - **Complaint to the Florida Governor’s Office:** Documented the hostile environment created by Governor DeSantis’s policies, which have emboldened law enforcement to criminalize and harass the homeless【93†source】. - **Complaint to Miami-Dade Internal Affairs:** Detailed repeated harassment by MDPD officers, threats of arrest, and improper enforcement of civil matters【94†source】. - **Complaint to the Florida Attorney General:** Highlighted systemic misconduct by MDPD, including over 20 instances of harassment and classist discrimination against homeless individuals【95†source】. - **Complaint to the Department of Justice Civil Rights Division:** Accused MDPD of violating civil rights through unlawful harassment, threats, and attempts at self-help evictions【99†source】. - **Complaint to the Miami-Dade Independent Civilian Panel:** Urged investigation into repeated misconduct by MDPD officers, including false threats of arrest and refusal to identify themselves【97†source】. - **Complaint to District 8 County Commissioner Danielle Cohen Higgins:** Emphasized continuous harassment, disregard for legal protocols, and failure to address violence against homeless individuals【98†source】. - **Complaint to the Assistant Miami-Dade State Attorney:** Framed MDPD’s repeated harassment as potential hate crimes, highlighting their hostility towards homeless individuals【100†source】. --- ### **Request for Intervention** We are requesting the following from the Miami-Dade County Homeless Trust: 1. **Immediate Investigation into New Hope C.O.R.P.S.** Investigate their misconduct, false promises, and illegal actions, including their collaboration with law enforcement in violating eviction laws. 2. **Temporary Shelter in a Hotel Until Permanent Housing is Secured** We are currently living in unsafe conditions beside a busy road, exposed to traffic, violence, and further harassment.

Furthermore, Florida law mandates court involvement for eviction proceedings, which was entirely bypassed in our case.

Our belongings were discarded by workers after Officer Cheney denied us access when we took a short break, causing us to lose essential items and suffer profound emotional distress. --- ### **Legal Violations and Misconduct** The actions taken against us constitute clear violations of Florida law: - **Unlawful Eviction:** The eviction lacked proper notice, court authorization, and involvement of certified process servers as required by Florida law. - **Unauthorized Practice:** Stephen Heath’s actions in posting the eviction notice exceeded his legal authority. - **Collusion and Misrepresentation:** New Hope’s collaboration with MDPD, under the guise of assistance, aimed to displace us without due process. --- ### **Corroborating Evidence from Formal Complaints** The following complaints corroborate our experiences and highlight systemic issues: - **Complaint to the Florida Governor’s Office:** Documented the hostile environment created by Governor DeSantis’s policies, which have emboldened law enforcement to criminalize and harass the homeless【93†source】. - **Complaint to Miami-Dade Internal Affairs:** Detailed repeated harassment by MDPD officers, threats of arrest, and improper enforcement of civil matters【94†source】. - **Complaint to the Florida Attorney General:** Highlighted systemic misconduct by MDPD, including over 20 instances of harassment and classist discrimination against homeless individuals【95†source】. - **Complaint to the Department of Justice Civil Rights Division:** Accused MDPD of violating civil rights through unlawful harassment, threats, and attempts at self-help evictions【99†source】. - **Complaint to the Miami-Dade Independent Civilian Panel:** Urged investigation into repeated misconduct by MDPD officers, including false threats of arrest and refusal to identify themselves【97†source】. - **Complaint to District 8 County Commissioner Danielle Cohen Higgins:** Emphasized continuous harassment, disregard for legal protocols, and failure to address violence against homeless individuals【98†source】. - **Complaint to the Assistant Miami-Dade State Attorney:** Framed MDPD’s repeated harassment as potential hate crimes, highlighting their hostility towards homeless individuals【100†source】. --- ### **Request for Intervention** We are requesting the following from the Miami-Dade County Homeless Trust: 1. **Immediate Investigation into New Hope C.O.R.P.S.** Investigate their misconduct, false promises, and illegal actions, including their collaboration with law enforcement in violating eviction laws. 2. **Temporary Shelter in a Hotel Until Permanent Housing is Secured** We are currently living in unsafe conditions beside a busy road, exposed to traffic, violence, and further harassment.

Temporary shelter is essential for our safety and well-being until permanent housing can be arranged. 3. **Accountability for Miami-Dade Police Department Officers Involved** Investigate the officers who participated in unlawful evictions, harassment, and threats, and hold them accountable. 4. **Mandatory Training on Florida’s Hate Crime Statutes** Educate outreach workers and law enforcement on protections for homeless individuals to prevent further rights violations. --- ### **Why We Call Them ‘No Hope’** New Hope C.O.R.P.S. presented itself as a beacon of hope but quickly revealed its true nature.

The Legislative Environment Under Governor Ron DeSantis** In March 2024, Governor Ron DeSantis signed HB 1365, a law that prohibits individuals from sleeping on sidewalks, in parks, on beaches, or other public spaces.

However, the implementation of this law has led to the criminalization of homelessness, effectively punishing individuals for their lack of housing rather than addressing the root causes of homelessness. citeturn0search0 The law provides a $30 million budget for municipalities to comply, but local officials argue it is insufficient to address the issue effectively.

The Dark Irony and Egregiousness of the Situation** The actions of New Hope C.O.R.P.S. are not only a betrayal of their mission but also highlight a dark irony: an organization designed to provide housing and support to the homeless is actively collaborating with law enforcement to displace and criminalize the very individuals it is meant to serve.

Conclusion** The systemic failures and rights violations we have endured are not isolated incidents but are indicative of a broader, policy-induced environment that criminalizes homelessness and undermines the efforts of those seeking assistance.

We urge the Miami-Dade County Homeless Trust to investigate New Hope C.O.R.P.S., hold them accountable for their failures, and advocate for policy reforms that prioritize the dignity and rights of homeless individuals.
